A: ICJ’s Genocide Verdict on Myanmar and the Ramifications for Israel.
B: On November 19, the International Court of Justice (ICJ) convicted Myanmar of genocide. As supporters of the Rohingya from Cox’s Bazar to The Hague wept and cheered with relief, legal experts were warning of a backlash against the world’s top court amid ramifications for Israel.
B: The British Institute of International and Comparative Law (BIICL) said some of the ICJ findings could be “particularly relevant to South Africa’s genocide case against Israel,” adding that “this judgment will have consequences far beyond Myanmar.” That case – accusing Israel of committing genocide against the Palestinians in Gaza – is currently in the written pleadings stage, with a trial widely expected in 2029.
B: The United Nations’ 1948 Genocide Convention lays out the definition of genocide as “acts committed with intent to destroy, in whole or in part, a national, ethnic, racial or religious group.” Israel rejects the genocide allegations, arguing its military campaign in Gaza targeted Hamas, not Palestinian civilians, and justified the carnage as self-defense in response to the October 7, 2023 attacks by Hamas.
B: It has rebuked the ICJ’s rulings as distorted or biased. Sonya Sceats, the BIICL’s chief executive officer, said a genocide committed by a state against its people – as Rohingya survivors testified – now has global consequences and the international courts deserve support.
B: “The tragedy of the backlash set to engulf the world’s top court after this ruling is that it has little to do with the Rohingya victims and survivors of genocide and everything to do with perceived implications for another brutal conflict thousands of miles away in the Middle East,” she said.
B: “How states of all sizes respond to any politicized attacks on the court will be a test of their commitment to international justice not just for responding to atrocities but also more generally as an essential means of settling differences peacefully with equality of arms before an independent court.” The ICJ heard that an estimated 10,000 Rohingya were killed in 2017 during what the Myanmar military described as a “clearance operations” and nearly 400 villages – about 40 percent of the almost 1,000 villages in three townships of northern Rakhine state – were partially or totally destroyed.

A: The ICJ’s Myanmar Genocide Ruling: What Comes Next?.
B: Yesterday, the International Court of Justice (ICJ) in The Hague ruled that Myanmar is responsible for having committed a genocide against the Rohingya Muslim minority in 2017.
B: The case was a landmark – the first time that a state was held responsible for genocide, and only the fourth time since the Holocaust that an international court ruled that the legal threshold to prove genocide had been met.
B: Seven years after the case was brought by The Gambia, the ICJ’s judges ruled, by a margin of nine votes to six, that thousands of killings and widespread sexual violence by Myanmar’s security forces against the Rohingya population in 2017 amounted to state-sponsored genocide.
B: “The court finds that Myanmar’s security forces killed thousands of Rohingya civilians, perpetrated widespread acts of sexual violence as well as acts of extreme violence against children and attacked fleeing Rohingya,” presiding Judge Yuji Iwasawa said in a reading of the decision.
B: The verdict has been widely praised by survivors and legal advocates alike.
B: Arsalan Suleman, the lead counsel for The Gambia, described the guilty verdict as “a landmark victory for the Rohingya people and for countless individuals in Myanmar who have endured violence at the hands of the military.” He added, “It represents an important step toward accountability and honors the courage and perseverance of the victims and survivors who have spent years seeking justice.” As of press time, Myanmar’s military-backed government, which took office in April, has not yet made any public comment about the verdict.
B: Nor has it received much mention in leading regime media outlets.
B: The front page of the Global New Light of Myanmar was instead taken up with reports of a meeting between Min Aung Hlaing and the head of the BIMSTEC economic bloc, Min Aung Hlaing’s donation of alms for the Buddhist Kathin festival, and “news” of engagements involving diplomats from Sri Lanka and Belarus.
B: Nonetheless, regime propaganda accounts have offered an indication of how Naypyidaw intends to respond to the landmark verdict.
B: In the run-up to the ruling, many pro-military Telegram channels changed their profile pictures to an image of a lighted torch imprinted with the colors of the Myanmar flag, emblazoned with the words “A Light For Justice.” In addition to attempting to spin the verdict as a qualified victory for Myanmar, one prominent pro-military account on X responded to the guilty verdict by posting AI-generated images highlighting gre

A: EU, Indonesia officially sign 'ambitious' trade deal.
B: EU, Indonesia officially sign 'ambitious' trade deal Brussels said the deal will boost economic growth, with the EU's GDP projected to be 16 billion euros higher and Indonesia's 27 billion euros higher by 2042 than they would be without the agreement.
B: BRUSSELS: The EU and Indonesia officially signed a trade deal to open up their markets in Brussels on Saturday (Nov 21), paving the way for the accord to come into force early next year.
B: EU chief Ursula von der Leyen and Indonesian Minister of Economic Affairs Airlangga Hartarto attended a signing ceremony for a pact that Brussels said is projected to increase European Union exports to Southeast Asia's largest economy by almost 40 per cent within 15 years.
B: "This is one of the most ambitious deals the European Union has concluded in Asia," said von der Leyen, the European Commission president. EU trade chief Maros Sefcovic and Indonesia's minister for trade Budi Santoso were also in attendance.
B: "Together, we represent a market of 730 million people," von der Leyen added, thanking Indonesian President Prabowo Subianto, who she said will visit Brussels on Dec 11.
B: Negotiations for the deal were concluded last year, almost a decade after they were launched, as part of a wider EU push to diversify trade as it faces challenges from the United States and China.
B: The Indonesia-European Union Comprehensive Economic Partnership Agreement (CEPA) and the ancillary Investment Protection Agreement (IPA) will open investment in strategic sectors such as electric vehicles, electronics, and pharmaceuticals, Brussels said.
B: The pact removes tariffs on more than 98 per cent of European exports to the world's fourth most populous country, including food, agricultural and industrial products and will help save over 600 million euros (US$658 million) a year in customs duties, according to the EU.
B: Around 90 per cent of Indonesian exports to the EU will be tariff-free, Airlangga said, noting that is expected to benefit the country's top shipments to the bloc including palm oil, footwear and textiles fisheries. "The benefits for our exporters are concrete and measurable," he said, hailing a "new chapter" in economic relations with the EU.
B: Palm oil and deforestation had posed stumbling blocks to the negotiations, and the pact includes mechanisms for cooperation on regulatory and sustainability issues as well as a dedicated protocol on palm oil to allay some of the related concerns. A: Indonesia fines and sanctions firms for stock manipulation, disclosure failures.
B: Indonesia fines and sanctions firms for stock manipulation, disclosure failures This comes amid efforts by Indonesia to strengthen enforcement, bolster market integrity and protect investors.
B: JAKARTA: Indonesia's Financial Services Authority (OJK) on Saturday (Nov 21) announced sanctions in 10 capital market violation cases, amid efforts to strengthen enforcement, bolster market integrity and protect investors.
B: OJK ordered the return of 23.77 billion rupiah (US$1.3 million) in illicit gains from three stock manipulation cases, involving shares of PT Grand House Mulia, PT Saraswanti Anugerah Makmur and PT Suryamas Dutamakmur.
B: In the Grand House Mulia case, OJK also ordered the closure of 15 securities accounts linked to the trading activity between March and July 2023.
B: The agency also imposed a total of 3.71 billion rupiah in administrative fines on seven listed companies for disclosure failures, material and affiliated-party transaction breaches, financial reporting violations and non-compliance with share transfer obligations.
B: OJK also barred multiple individuals involved in the manipulation cases from participating in Indonesia's capital markets, derivatives and carbon exchange sectors for periods ranging from three to 10 years.
B: OJK previously sanctioned three listed firms as part of Indonesia's market reforms following concerns raised by global index providers over the transparency and investability of Indonesia's capital markets.
A: Rohingya in Bangladesh welcome UN court’s genocide verdict but say they want to go home to Myanmar.
B: Rohingya in Bangladesh welcome UN court’s genocide verdict but say they want to go home to Myanmar It was a landmark judgment, finding Myanmar responsible for the crime against the country’s ethnic Rohingya - the first time the court ruled that a state committed genocide.
B: COX’S BAZAR, Bangladesh: Hundreds of Rohingya refugees in Bangladesh sat in front of a big screen at their sprawling camp in Cox’s Bazar, watching the news from the Netherlands, where the UN's top court ruled that Myanmar committed genocide against the Rohingya minority in a brutal 2017 military operation.
B: For many, the historic verdict on Thursday (Nov 19) represented a “small victory” - offering a glimmer of hope that someday they would be able to return safely to their homes in Myanmar, across the border from Bangladesh. Only then, some said, will they be “fully satisfied”.
B: It was a landmark judgment, finding Myanmar responsible for the crime against the country’s ethnic Rohingya - the first time the court ruled that a state committed genocide.
B: The Hague-based International Court of Justice also ordered Myanmar’s military-supported government to allow the Rohingya to return home safely, pay reparations to the Rohingya Muslims and prosecute those suspected of having committed genocide. But it remains to be seen how much of the ruling can be implemented.
B: The court’s rulings are final and legally binding, but it has no way to enforce them beyond reporting non-compliance to the UN Security Council. Myanmar’s government on Friday dismissed the ruling as “unfair”.
B: Bangladesh Foreign Minister Khalilur Rahman, who is also president of the UN General Assembly, went to Cox’s Bazar on Friday to tell Rohingyas who fled the genocide in Myanmar that “at this historic moment” there must be action, his UN spokesperson said.
B: Suspects must be prosecuted, Rohingya must be able to return to Myanmar voluntarily and safely, they must receive reparations and Myanmar’s government must ensure there will never be another genocide, Rahman said, according to spokesperson Melissa Summers.
B: He also said he will advocate with UN member nations on how to follow up on the judgment, stressing “You will not be alone,” she said. At the refugee camp in Cox’s Bazar on Thursday, Rohingya refugees told The Associated Press of their concerns.
